Let me think about the proof approach.

This is a classic noninterference proof for a security-typed STLC-like calculus with locally nameless syntax. The standard approach (from "Security Types Preserve Confidentiality" / the Penn STLC noninterference proof, e.g., the `stlc-noninterference` benchmark in the "Proof Engineering" benchmark suite) uses a logical relation with a "world" / environment-based indistinguishability.

Let me recall the standard proof structure from the STLC noninterference benchmark (from the PRL/DeepSpec benchmarks, based on the "Noninterference" exercise of Software Foundations Vol 4? Actually it's from the paper "The Next 700 Semantics" or the benchmark from "A benchmark of noninterference proofs"?). Let me recall: the file `stlc-noninterference` in the "benchmarks" repository (e.g., `pl-foundations/benchmarks`?) There's the famous "noninterference" proof in the style of the "Security" chapter.

The key structure:
- Define `related` logical relation on terms: two terms are related at type T if they either both diverge or both terminate with related values.
- Define `related_values` on values.
- Closure under anti-reduction (the "step" relation, with erase-security commuting).
- Fundamental theorem: if `has_type Gamma t T` and the environments `gamma1 ~ gamma2` (relating substitutions pointwise), then substitutions of t are related.

But here we have security annotations, protection, and the notion of "same_result" after erase_security. The theorem is stated with the "context" approach (plug C t1 vs plug C t2) rather than the "two different stores/env" approach, so the environments are empty and t1, t2 are closed terms of the same type T_secret. The relation is "same_result" at erased level.

Hmm, the standard approach for this: define a relation `interp (kappa : security) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop` meaning "t1 and t2 are indistinguishable at type T with respect to observer level..." Hmm.

Let me think about what's actually needed. The theorem says: t1, t2 both closed with type T_secret. C is a typed context. If the indirect_reader of T_secret can't flow to the indirect_reader of T_result, and T_result is ground and transparent, then plug C t1 and plug C t2 have equal results after erasure.

Note that t1 and t2 are arbitrary closed terms of the same secret type — they can be different terms. The key property: results observed at level T_result's indirect_reader can't depend on the secret, whose indirect_reader is High and can't flow to result's indirect_reader (which would be Low). So essentially T_result's indirect reader must be Low while T_secret's indirect_reader is High (or result's indirect reader is High too, in which case... wait, flows_to High Low = False, so the constraint means not (T_secret indirect = High and T_result indirect = Low). Since if T_secret indirect = Low, flows_to Low l = True always, so no constraint... hmm, but then t1 and t2 have the same "secret type". Hmm.

Wait, "same secret type" — the type T_secret could have Low indirect reader, which means both t1 and t2 are public... no wait, they just have the same type. The noninterference statement: when T_secret's indirect reader is High (i.e., the term is secret in terms of indirect reading), and T_result's is Low, results equal.

Actually the classic approach: the logical relation is indexed by the security level (observer level) kappa. Two terms are related at type T w.r.t. level kappa if ... Actually let me search: the definitions `security`, `reader`, `indirect_reader`, `flows_to`, `protect_security`, `ty_protect`, `tm_protect`, `erase_security`, `same_result`, `transparent` — this looks like the "Noninterference" benchmark of the "Benchmarks" used in the paper "Proof Repair"? Doesn't matter.

The key: I need to construct a logical relation and prove fundamental theorem. Let me think carefully about the semantics.

Language:
- `tm_if c yes no r`: evaluate c to `tm_inl v k` or `tm_inr v k`, then protect the corresponding branch by k.indirect_reader. Note: it doesn't open a binder; `yes` and `no` are closed terms directly (typed at T in the same Gamma). Then result is `tm_protect k.($indirect) yes`.
- `T_If` gives type `ty_protect k.indirect T`.

So this is like an "if" where branches are pre-evaluated terms.

- `tm_case t body1 body2 r`: evaluates t, then opens body with the value; protect by k.indirect.
- Application: result protected by kappa.indirect where kappa is function type's security (the arrow's own label).

Hmm wait, look at ST_AppAbs: `tm_app (tm_abs T body kappa) v r --> tm_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) (open body v)`. But the typing T_App: `has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa)` ... result `ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2`. So the arrow's security kappa contains both reader and indirect_reader of the function. Interesting: `flows_to kappa.reader r` is required for the step (the label `r` at application is the "reader level" of the context?).

Hmm, the relation must deal with these protections and the fact that a term's *type*'s security records the reader/indirect_reader levels; and protection changes the security of values.

Let me try to recall/derive the right logical relation. This resembles the "Security" formalization of the SLam calculus (Heintze & Riecke) or the "noninterference for STLC" benchmark where the LR is:

`interp : security -> ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop`? Hmm.

Termination-insensitive.

Standard technique: Define `same_result`-based logical relation where every type is interpreted as "same_result" (since the result is erased) plus appropriate constraints on security levels. Actually, for the context theorem, we need the fundamental theorem: if `has_type Gamma t T` and `gamma1`, `gamma2` are "related" at Gamma (with respect to some observer level), then `t[gamma1]` and `t[gamma2]` are related at T.

But here the theorem uses the *same* substitutions t1 and t2 with empty context, plugged into a context C. So we can convert it to environment-based: `plug C t` = `t` substituted... hmm, context C with a hole binds variables via C_Abs/C_Case; the plugging is substitution of the hole's term into the context, where the hole is under binders (C_Abs introduces a binder around the hole, C_CaseLeft/Right too). So `plug C t1` vs `plug C t2` are terms where t1 and t2 appear in the same places under the same binders. So it's really a "relational" property: if t1 ~ t2 at T_secret (which holds since both are closed and typed... need both have same erased behavior!), then plug C t1 ~ plug C t2 at T_result.

Wait, but t1 and t2 can be arbitrary closed terms of type T_secret. When are they related? They need to produce the same result when observed at level... Since T_secret's indirect reader is High (in the interesting case) and result observer is Low, we need: for any t1, t2 of type T_secret, plug C t1 and plug C t2 produce equal erased results when the result type is transparent (i.e., all security levels in T_result are <= its own security... `transparent T_result` means `transparent_at (security_of T_result) T_result`, i.e., every subterm type's security is <= T_result's security pointwise: reader flow and indirect flow). Hmm.

So the idea: The whole result T_result's `security_of` has indirect_reader Low (when T_secret's indirect is High). And transparency means subparts have security_le to the top-level security. Since result type's reader/indirect are Low, every subpart's reader and indirect reader are Low. So erased values are... hmm, `erase_security` erases everything to `public`, so equality of erased values is equality of the "skeleton" of values.

Hmm, so what does the logical relation need to capture? Since all terms in the relation get erased, and the type's security levels say what an observer can learn, we need:

At type T with security kappa, terms t1 and t2 are related if, roughly, they have the same "public behavior" when observed... but since both t1 t2 are given (not originating from different secrets), the relation is between two terms both typed at T. The fundamental theorem needs: closed terms of type T are related to themselves? No — t1 and t2 both have type T_secret but are different terms. They should be related at T_secret because T_secret's indirect reader is High (secret) — i.e., the observer is at Low. So relation at T w.r.t. observer level: if the term's security indirect_reader is High, then anything of that type is related to anything? That can't be right for the context to work... wait.

Let's think: a context C typed at T_secret -> T_result. If t1, t2 are both of type T_secret with High indirect reader and result is Low-observable, then plug C t1 and plug C t2 must have equal results. But C could be `C_Hole` itself! Then T_result = T_secret, and we'd need `~ flows_to High High`, i.e. False — but flows_to High High = True. So C_Hole is excluded by the hypothesis. Good.

If C is e.g. `C_AppLeft C' t2 r`... hmm the context has the hole in a position and the typing ensures the context is well-typed at the hole type.

OK here's the classical setup for such a theorem: The relation is defined between two *programs* (or two substitutions) at a type, parameterized by a level. Actually the standard noninterference theorem for such calculi: Given two terms t1, t2 of type T in the same context, if they're "equivalent" then plugging into any typed context gives equivalent results. And the equivalence at a type is defined as: t1 ~ t2 iff for all observers at level... hmm.

Let me look at this from the "low equivalence" perspective. Classic approach in SLam noninterference (Heintze-Riecke, and the implementation in Coq by ... e.g. "Noninterference for a simply typed lambda calculus with references"): define a logical relation `~_T` where `t1 ~_T t2` iff their evaluations both terminate with values v1, v2 with `v1 ~^val_T v2`, OR both diverge. Values related at base type T if their security level is High (then always related) or their "observable parts" are equal.

For this task, since `same_result` uses `erase_security` on the final *values*, and types have security annotations, the relation probably needs to be: at type T, t1 and t2 are related (w.r.t. some "ambient level" or not) iff ... hmm.

Now let me think carefully about the proof.

Let me set up notation mentally:
- `security := { reader : label; indirect_reader : label }`.
- `flows_to l1 l2` is False only when l1 = High, l2 = Low.
- `join l1 l2` = High unless both Low.
- `protect_security kappa l` joins l into both.
- `security_le k1 k2 := flows_to k1.reader k2.reader /\ flows_to k1.indirect_reader k2.indirect_reader`.

- Types: `Ty_Unit kappa`, `Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa`, `Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa`, `Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa`.
- `ty_protect l T` protects the top-level security.
- `wf_security k := flows_to k.indirect_reader k.reader` — meaning the indirect reader can't be more restrictive? Wait, wf means indirect_reader ≤ reader in the flow order. flows_to High Low = False. So if indirect_reader = High and reader = Low it's ill-formed. I.e., indirect reader must flow to reader: the "reader" is the level of direct reading and "indirect_reader" is the level of indirect (implicit) flows, and wf requires indirect flows to be allowed to flow into direct readers... Hmm, actually in SLam, `κ = (r, ir)` with `ir ⊑ r` meaning anything that can be indirectly read at ir can be directly read at r. So "reader" is the permitted level and indirect_reader is what's tainted.

- The typing rules: applications require `flows_to kappa.reader r` where kappa is the arrow's security; the result is protected with `kappa.indirect_reader`. So if the function type has security `(reader=r1, indirect=ir1)` and we apply at label `r`, then the result is protected at ir1, and the step requires r1 flows_to r (i.e., if r1 = High then r = High).

Hmm right, this matches SLam's application rule: Γ ⊢ e1 : τ1 --κ--> τ2, Γ ⊢ e2 : τ1, κ = (r1, ir1), and the application label r with r1 ⊑ r; result type τ2 protected at ir1.

- Protection `tm_protect l t`: protects the *value* by joining l into its security (both reader and indirect_reader). Typing: `T_Protect` gives `ty_protect l T`.

- if-then-else: `T_If` requires the condition to have type `Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k`, branches type T, and `flows_to k.reader r` where `r` is the if's label; result `ty_protect k.indirect_reader T`. Step: `tm_if (tm_inl v k) yes no r --> tm_protect k.indirect_reader yes`.

Now, the target theorem. Key observation: t1 and t2 are closed terms of type T_secret; context C is typed at T_secret -> T_result.

We need to show same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2): for any values v1, v2 with plug C t1 -->* v1 and plug C t2 -->* v2, erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

Since T_result is ground and transparent, and `~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).indirect (security_of T_result).indirect`.

Hmm, so what's the actual mathematical content? Terms t1, t2 of type T_secret — both arbitrary. When can they influence the result? The result's indirect_reader is at least... wait: the constraint `~flows_to secret_ir result_ir` means secret_ir=High and result_ir=Low. Under wf assumptions (types are wf), can T_result have reader Low and indirect_reader Low while some subterm has High? `transparent T_result` requires every subterm's security ≤ security_of T_result. So all security annotations in T_result are Low-ish: reader flows to Low means reader=Low; indirect flows to Low means indirect=Low. So every type in T_result has security ≤ (Low,Low) i.e. exactly public-ish? Actually security_le k public means k.reader flows to Low => k.reader=Low, and k.indirect flows to Low => k.indirect=Low. So every security in T_result is (Low,Low) = public.

Given transparency and groundness: T_result is a "public" type: all its security annotations are Low.

Hmm wait, but transparent_at requires security_le (security_of T) kappa at each node where kappa is the *top-level* security. So yes.

So the conclusion: any two closed t1, t2 of secret type produce equal erased results in the context, when the result type is fully public (all levels Low) and ground.

This means: the context, when applied to any term of type T_secret, produces a *public* result whose erased value is the same regardless of t1 vs t2. That's because T_secret's indirect_reader is High: the whole secret term is an indirect (implicit) flow source at level High; since the result's levels are all Low, the typing must prevent the secret from flowing into anything observable. So the result's erased skeleton is determined regardless.

Wait, but is that actually true? Consider T_secret = Ty_Unit public? No: then secret_ir = Low, and flows_to Low result_ir = True, so hypothesis `~ flows_to Low Low` = False — vacuous. So the interesting case is secret_ir = High. Since T_secret is well-formed presumably (has_type empty t1 T_secret implies wf_ty T_secret? Let's check: has_type gives wf_ty in the T_Var case only... Hmm, let's think about the actual proof.

Anyway, let's devise the proof ourselves.

Approach: Define an "erasure-based" logical relation.

Since the final observation is on erased values, we need the relation at a type to account for the security annotations. Intuition from SLam noninterference proofs:

Define `interp (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop` (values related at T), parameterized perhaps by an observer level. Standard SLam:
- For base types (unit, sums, prods), values are related if their observable components are related, where observability depends on the security label: if `security_of T` has reader/indirect... hmm.

Actually, in SLam's noninterference, the relation at type τ with security κ is: two values are related at (τ, κ) if ... the standard formulation uses "levels": the relation `v1 ~^κ_τ v2` where κ is the current "observer level" (the level of the context). For a base type T with security κ_T, the rule is:
- if κ_T ⊑ κ (i.e., the type's reader is below the observer level) then the values must be equal (?), else anything.

Hmm, but here we're comparing values of the same closed type with security annotations. Let me think about what property suffices.

Alternative approach: since we only need equality of erased results (and erase_security destroys all security info and all type info, keeping only the term structure), maybe we can define the relation in terms of erased terms and the *security levels*. Hmm, but the values could be closures; erase_security of a closure `tm_abs T body kappa` is `tm_abs (erase_type T) (erase_security body) public` — so erased closures compare the erased bodies.

Important: erase_security of values under protection: `protect_value v l` changes the security annotation of the value's top node. `erase_security (protect_value v l) = erase_security v`? Let's check: for `tm_unit kappa`, protect gives `tm_unit (protect_security kappa l)`, erase both = `tm_unit public`. For abs: erase (tm_abs T body k) = tm_abs (erase_type T) (erase body) public; protected = tm_abs T body (protect_security k l) → erase same. For pair: `tm_pair v1 v2 kappa` vs protected: `tm_pair v1 v2 (protect_security kappa l)`: erase both `tm_pair (erase v1) (erase v2) public`. Inl/inr similar. So `erase_security (protect_value v l) = erase_security v` for values v. 

Now the key semantic fact: if `t1 --> t2` then `erase_security t1 --> ...`? Not exactly, because step requires `flows_to kappa.reader r`, which erased terms may not satisfy? Erasure sets all labels to Low/public, and flows_to Low Low = True; flows_to public.reader Low? public.reader = Low, so flows_to Low Low = True. And for erased terms, all security annotations are public: reader=Low, indirect=Low. So the side conditions hold. So erasure maps steps to steps, but maybe not exactly since `erase_security (tm_protect l t) = erase_security t`, so a step `tm_app (tm_abs T body k) v r --> tm_protect k.ir (open body v)` maps to `tm_app (tm_abs (erase_type T) (erase body) public) (erase v) Low --> erase (open body v)`. And `open (erase body) (erase v) = erase (open body v)`? Need `erase_security (open body v) = open (erase_security body) (erase_security v)`. That's the standard substitution/opening commutation lemma. Also `tm_protect` erase = erase of body. So yes, erased step corresponds to an erased step. And ST_ProtectValue: `tm_protect l v --> protect_value v l` erases to `erase v --> erase v`, which is a step? `tm_protect` erased away, so the erased version would be `erase v -->* erase v`? Hmm, `erase_security (tm_protect l v) = erase_security v` and `erase_security (protect_value v l) = erase_security v`, so this step maps to a reflexive multi-step, not a single step. Fine: multi-step erasure: `t -->* v` implies `erase t -->* erase v`. Because each step either maps to a step or to refl. Good.

Hmm, but actually maybe we don't need the erasure simulation at all, since `same_result` is directly about erased values.

Let's think about the structure of the proof. We need a relation R_T between terms such that:
1. (Fundamental) If `has_type Gamma t T`, and environments γ1, γ2 are related at Gamma (pointwise R at each type, possibly with an environment-level condition), then `t[γ1]` and `t[γ2]` are related at T (in the "same_result"-like sense).
2. The relation at T with the right security condition implies equality of erased results.
3. The hypothesis gives us: t1 and t2 are related at T_secret (with empty env).
4. From context_has_type C T_secret T_result, derive that plug C t1 and plug C t2 are related at T_result, and with the extra conditions (ground, transparent, no-flow), conclude same_result.

Point 4 is actually just the fundamental theorem applied with an environment mapping a fresh variable x to T_secret: the context C is plugged with tm_fvar x, typed in `update empty x T_secret`. The composition lemma: if `x ⊢ plug C x : T_result` and `t1, t2` are related at T_secret, then `plug C t1` and `plug C t2` related at T_result? That requires substitution lemma for the relation. Hmm, but the context's typing holds only for x not in L (a finite set), and we plug in t1/t2 which are closed but may contain fvar names — closed means fv = [], so no clash. But wait — `context_has_type` is stated with `plug C (tm_fvar x)` and requires x ∉ L. And t1, t2 are `closed` (has_type empty implies closed? Not necessarily fv = []? has_type empty t T: T_Var requires lookup_context x empty = Some T, impossible since lookup in [] = None. So terms typed in empty context have no free fvars. Good. But also the context C may bind x? No, x is fresh, and C's binders are anonymous locally nameless binders; plug puts t under binders. But t is locally closed (lc_at 0)? has_type empty t T should imply locally_closed t. We may need to prove those properties, or avoid them by using a "semantic" composition.

Hmm, however: the fundamental theorem with contexts usually is proven via "if has_type Gamma t T and γ1 ~ γ2 : Gamma then t[γ1] ~ t[γ2] : T". Then for a program context C, `plug C t` = the term C with hole replaced. We can view this as substitution of t for the free variable x... but `plug` doesn't substitute; it's syntactic replacement where under binders the hole stays at binder depth 0? Let's check: `plug (C_Abs T C' kappa) t = tm_abs T (plug C' t) kappa`. So plugging under a binder does NOT shift. Since t is locally closed, that's fine, but it means plug C (tm_fvar x) is exactly a term where x is free (if x doesn't appear in C otherwise), and plug C t = subst x t (plug C (tm_fvar x)) provided x ∉ fv of things in C. Fine.

Alternatively, we can define the relation on *open* terms with environments and prove the fundamental theorem, then handle the context by relating the environment maps. The standard approach:

Define `sem_env (Gamma : context) (gamma1 gamma2 : list (atom * tm))` or substitution functions... Hmm, locally nameless with fvars: a term with free variables can be "substituted". Let's think about what the relation should be exactly. This is the crux.

Let me think about the semantics of security levels. The final theorem: result type is ground and transparent, i.e., fully Low. The secret type has indirect_reader High. Consider the simplest case: C = C_Hole would give T_result = T_secret, excluded. C = C_Protect High C_Hole gives T_result = ty_protect High T_secret, whose indirect_reader is High — excluded? flows_to High High = True, so ¬ flows_to High High is False → vacuous. OK.

C = C_AppLeft C' t2 r where the hole is the function position... Then T_secret must be an arrow type (or subtype). The arrow's security kappa must satisfy flows_to kappa.reader r. And the result type is ty_protect kappa.indirect T2. For T_result to have indirect_reader Low (since secret_ir High, must be that result_ir Low → kappa.indirect must be Low... wait ty_protect kappa.ir T2's indirect = join T2.ir kappa.ir; result_ir Low means T2.ir=Low and kappa.ir=Low). But the secret's indirect_reader = kappa.ir = Low, contradicting secret_ir = High. Hmm interesting: so if T_secret = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa with kappa.indirect = Low, then secret_ir = Low, and the hypothesis is vacuous. So the non-vacuous cases are when secret types have High indirect readers.

Let's consider concretely: t1, t2 : Ty_Unit (High,High) (a secret unit). Contexts: C = C_Protect l C_Hole with result ty_protect l (Ty_Unit secret) — indirect joins High with l = High; not Low, so vacuous unless... flows_to High High = True, so hypothesis false. OK.

What about C = C_Fst C' r, where C' : context for the pair? T_secret must be a product type Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa. Then result type = ty_protect kappa.ir T1. For result to have Low indirect, need T1.ir Low and kappa.ir Low. But secret type's indirect = kappa.ir = High. Contradiction. Vacuuous.

Hmm, so wait: the secret type's indirect reader is High, and the context result's is Low, and the typing rules propagate indirect_reader of the type of the hole... It seems in many cases the flow is forced. I'll design the relation to be "indistinguishability at observer level Low (or parameterized by level l)". Let's parameterize by a level `l` (the current observation level). Define:

`RV (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop` — values v1, v2 of type T are indistinguishable to an observer at level l.

Now the fundamental theorem. Let's set it up with environments.

Because terms are locally nameless with named free variables (fvar), the standard "open" relation: `R l T t1 t2` for terms with free variables, where the relation is defined semantically via closing substitutions? The usual approach for locally nameless: define the value relation `RV l T v1 v2` on closed values (or values with free variables, but then the closure bodies are open...). Hmm. In locally nameless, a closure `tm_abs T body κ` has `body` locally closed relative to 1 binder. Its relation should be defined via opening with related values: `forall a1 a2, RV l T1 a1 a2 -> R l T2 (open body a1) (open body a2)`. But `body` might contain free fvars too (from the context). So the relation must be defined for arbitrary terms with free variables, and the environment handles them. The environment-based approach: define

`R l T (gamma1 gamma2 : atom -> tm)`? Hmm, but Gamma is a context and substitutions map variables to terms.

Standard technique (from the "locally nameless" STLC noninterference benchmarks): define
```
Definition env_rel (l : label) (Gamma : context) (gamma1 gamma2 : list (atom * tm)) : Prop :=
  forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> 
    exists t1 t2, lookup gamma1 x = Some t1 /\ lookup gamma2 x = Some t2 /\ R l T t1 t2
```
or use total substitutions `atom -> tm` and require for x with type T: R l T (γ1 x) (γ2 x).

But then the fundamental theorem: if has_type Gamma t T and gamma1 ~ gamma2 at Gamma, then R l T (subst_all gamma1 t) (subst_all gamma2 t). Need: plug C t = subst x t (plug C (tm_fvar x)) when x ∉ (all names in C) and t closed. Hmm, careful: the substitution inside C's binders is fine because locally nameless binders are bvars; subst x only replaces free fvars. But C contains terms (in C_AppLeft etc.) which may contain fvar x? The context_has_type gives us that x is fresh from a list L, presumably L includes all fvars in C's embedded terms. So for a fresh x, x doesn't occur in the embedded terms, but plug C (tm_fvar x) has x free exactly at the hole. Then subst x t (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C t? First, let's characterize the semantic invariant we need. The final theorem says: for a closed term `plug C x` typed at T_result with a fresh variable x : T_secret, substituting two closed terms t1, t2 of type T_secret gives equal erased results, provided T_secret.ir ↛ T_result.ir and T_result ground+transparent.

Generalization: define for each type T and each "ambient level" l, a relation `R l T t1 t2` such that:
(A) Fundamental: if Γ ⊢ t : T, and env1 ~_l env2 at Γ, then R l T (t[env1]) (t[env2]).
(B) Final: if T is ground and transparent, R Low T t1 t2 implies same_result t1 t2.
(C) The hole: R Low T_secret t1 t2 holds for the given t1,t2 (using secret_ir = High and wf).

And the environment relation: env1 ~_l env2 at Γ: for all x T, Γ(x) = T → R l T (env1 x) (env2 x).

For (A) to go through, the relation must be compositional w.r.t. all typing rules. The tricky T_If/T_Case rules where the branch/scrutinee levels differ.

Let me try to define the relation with the following intuition: `R l T t1 t2` means "from the perspective of an observer at level l, t1 and t2 are indistinguishable". If l = Low, the observer sees only Low-readable stuff. If l = High, the observer sees everything readable at High, i.e., everything (all types wf → reader ⊑ High).

But then R High should be a strong relation for all terms (as strong as possible), and R Low is weaker. Is R monotone in l? If l' ⊑ l (i.e., l' lower), an observer at l' sees less, so R l' should be weaker: R l T ⊆ R l' T? Hmm, "weaker" meaning fewer constraints. If R High is strong, then R Low ⊆ R High (more pairs related at Low). Let's check: at type T with reader High: R Low = True, R High = strong. So R Low ⊇ R High ✓ (R Low is weaker). At type T with reader Low: both l=Low and l=High have flows_to Low l = True, so same strong relation. So monotonicity holds by the "if readable" definition: readable at l means flows_to reader l; since flows_to is transitive and Low ⊑ High... reads: if reader ⊑ Low then reader ⊑ High. So readable at Low implies readable at High. Thus strong-at-Low → strong-at-High, and at High additional types become readable (reader High) with strong relation. So R Low ⊇ R High? At reader-High types: R Low True (weak), R High strong (strong) ✓. At reader-Low: equal. So R Low is weaker or equal. Good.

Now the fundamental theorem at level l: subterms' relations at level l. The relation on terms of type T at level... Since both terms may have High reader, the observer at Low can't read them, so no constraint. The context uses the hole in a typed way. This is essentially the classic "noninterference via logical relation over a contextual plug".

Let me think about the relation from the "transparent" definition. `transparent T := transparent_at (security_of T) T`, where `transparent_at kappa T := security_le (security_of T) kappa /\ ...`. So transparency means every node's security ≤ the root's security. The theorem assumes result transparent (and ground). And the no-flow condition says secret_ir ↛ result_ir.

Since T_result is transparent, security_of T_result = κ with all subparts ≤ κ. The no-flow condition: if secret_ir = Low, flows_to Low result_ir is always True, contradiction; so secret_ir = High and result_ir = Low. Then transparency forces every security in T_result to have ir Low and reader Low (security_le to κ means flows_to reader κ.reader=Low → reader Low; flows_to ir Low → ir Low). So T_result is a fully public ground type. same_result demands erased equality.

Now the relation: I'll aim to prove a "low-indistinguishability" relation R T t1 t2 (no level parameter; observer always Low) with:

- RV T v1 v2:

  - if T is ground: if security_of T.reader = Low then require structural relation (which implies erased equality when transparent?); else True.

Hmm, let me think about whether to define RV as an inductive or a Fixpoint. Coq Fixpoint on ty is fine (ty is an inductive with recursive occurrences in all constructors; a Fixpoint returning Prop works if recursive calls are on subterms).

Let me now write the definitions concretely and check each typing rule.

Definitions:

```
Definition is_high (l : label) : Prop := l = High.  (* or use flows_to *)
Definition weak_ty (T : ty) : Prop := (security_of T).(indirect_reader) = High.
```

Since label has 2 constructors, I can use booleans or Props with decidability. Using `= Low` vs `= High` with case analysis is fine.

Let me define RV by recursion on T.

```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit kappa => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          (exists w1 w2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inl w1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl w2 ka2 /\ RV T1 w1 w2)
       \/ (exists w1 w2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_inr w1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr w2 ka2 /\ RV T2 w1 w2)
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low => exists w1 w2 ka1 ka2, v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 ka1 /\ ... /\ RV T1 ... /\ RV T2 ...
      | High => True
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | Low =>
          exists T1a b1 T1b b2 ka, v1 = tm_abs T1a b1 ka /\ v2 = tm_abs T1b b2 ka /\
          (forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T1 a1 a2 -> R T2 (open b1 a1) (open b2 a2))
      | High => True
      end
  end
with R (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2.
```

Wait, but the pair case: should it be `exists w1 w2 ka, v1 = tm_pair w1 w2 ka /\ v2 = tm_pair w1 w2 ka ...`? The annotations on the two pair values: same source term substituted → same annotation ka. But the components w1, w2 differ. Hmm, for products, the pair's annotation: the two sides have the same syntactic annotation ka. So requiring the same ka is fine. For sums similarly same annotation ka. But is that necessary? Let's keep annotations as variables but not require equality, to make the relation more robust; would that break anything? The runtime protection at case/if uses the *value's* annotation κa (the inl's annotation). Now, the fundamental theorem. Let me define the environment relation precisely.

We work with total substitutions `sigma : atom -> tm`. Define:

```
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma1 x)) /\
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma2 x)) /\
  forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R T (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x).
```

Hmm, the lc condition for all atoms is strong but true for our envs: sigma x is either t1/t2 (lc) or fvar y (lc) or arbitrary default for missing variables (can choose fvar 0? There's no atom "default"; we can define sigma as returning tm_unit public for unspecified atoms! tm_unit is lc ✓). Or define env as a list-based lookup with a default. Simpler: use total functions with a default unit.

Actually careful: the fundamental theorem's induction when a variable is not in Γ: the term can't be typed, so no constraint. Using total functions is convenient.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: the substitution `subst_all sigma t` replaces all fvars in t, including those not in Γ. Since t is typed in Γ, all its fvars are in Γ (by T_Var), so it doesn't matter ✓.

Alternatively, define env as an association list and use a lookup with default. Total functions are easier for the extension.

Now the fundamental theorem statement:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  wf_context Gamma ->
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2, env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
  R T (subst_all sigma1 t) (subst_all sigma2 t).
```

Now the arrow RV definition again, with the annotation condition:

```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
    match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
    | High => True
    | Low =>
        exists T1a b1 k1 T1b b2 k2,
          v1 = tm_abs T1a b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T1b b2 k2 /\
          k1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ k2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
          forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T1 a1 a2 ->
             R T2 (open b1 a1) (open b2 a2)
    end
```

This is a lot of "evaluation decomposition" lemmas. Let me think about how to structure them to minimize pain. Common approach: prove generalized "multi step inversion" lemmas of the form:

```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall t1 t2 r v,
  tm_app t1 t2 r -->* v ->
  (exists t1', t1 --> t1' /\ tm_app t1' t2 r -->* v) \/
  (exists w1 t2', value w1 /\ t1 = ... )
```
Hmm, better to follow the standard "step_deterministic / multi" style.

Alternative approach: define the relation R in terms of a *single-step simulation* rather than evaluation decomposition? Standard "logical relation is closed under steps" formulation:

Define R by coinduction-ish: `R T t1 t2 := (both value and RV) \/ (exists t1' t2', t1 --> t1' /\ t2 --> t2' /\ R T t1' t2')`? For termination-insensitive NI, the usual relation is: R T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> ... which is what we have. The decomposition lemmas are needed anyway.

Alternatively, define R semantically but with the evaluation decomposition done once per construct via a general "multi inversion" lemma:

For multi: `multi t v` with v a value. Prove inversion lemmas:
- `multi_app_inv`: multi (tm_app t1 t2 r) v → exists t1' t2' r', ... hmm.

Let me think about a cleaner way to handle the app case. Standard technique (from Software Foundations / TAPL): use "step_deterministic" plus inversion on multi. Actually, since our goal is just to relate values reached by arbitrary evaluations, maybe we can prove the decomposition in the following form:

```
Lemma evaluates_app : forall t1 t2 r v,
  evaluates (tm_app t1 t2 r) v ->
  exists w1 w2, 
    evaluates t1 w1 /\ evaluates t2 w2 /\
    (exists T1a body ka, w1 = tm_abs T1a body ka /\ 
       exists u, open body w2 -->* u /\ ... )
```
Hmm, the app steps: after w1 = tm_abs T1 body ka and w2 = a (value), ST_AppAbs gives tm_protect ka.ir (open body a); then the multi continues to v. So:

```
exists w1 a, evaluates t1 w1 /\ evaluates t2 a /\
  exists T1a body ka, w1 = tm_abs T1a body ka /\
  evaluates (tm_protect ka.ir (open body a)) v
```

Proving this by induction on the multi derivation is feasible:
```
Lemma app_eval_inv : forall t1 t2 r v,
  tm_app t1 t2 r -->* v -> value v ->
  exists w1 a, t1 -->* w1 /\ value w1 /\ t2 -->* a /\ value a /\
    exists T1a body ka, w1 = tm_abs T1a body ka /\
      tm_protect ka.ir (open body a) -->* v.
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r v H. remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as t eqn:E.
  induction H; subst...
```
Hmm, induction on the multi derivation with the term general. Alternative: prove by induction on the structure of the multi from the app:
- multi_refl: app t1 t2 r = v and value v — impossible since the app isn't a value (need lemma).
- multi_step: app t1 t2 r --> u and u -->* v. Invert the step:
  - ST_AppAbs: t1 = tm_abs ..., t2 = v (value), u = tm_protect ... (open ...). We need w1 = tm_abs... itself: t1 -->* t1 refl, t2 -->* t2 refl, and tm_protect ka.ir (open body a) -->* v given ✓.
  - ST_App1: t1 --> t1', u = tm_app t1' t2 r. Then by IH on u -->* v: exists w1 a ... t1' -->* w1 ... then t1 -->* w1 by multi_step ✓.
  - ST_App2: value t1, t2 --> t2', u = tm_app t1 t2' r. IH gives t2' -->* a, so t2 -->* a ✓, and w1 = t1 with t1 -->* t1 refl and value t1 ✓.

This works. We'll need similar for fst, snd, case, if, protect. And also "value_no_step"/"no_step_value" style inversion.

Now, let's think about whether the R relation is well-defined as a Fixpoint in Coq. R and RV are mutually recursive:
- RV on T matches on T; for arrows, calls R T2 (a subterm? T2 is a subterm of Arrow T1 T2 κ ✓) and RV T1 ✓.
- R calls RV T ✓.
So a mutual Fixpoint on ty works: 
```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with ... R T2 ... end
with R (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  fun t1 t2 => forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2.
```
Hmm, Fixpoint with mutual recursion where one returns a function: Coq syntax:
```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop := ...
with R (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2.
```
For the mutual recursion, the recursive calls RV T1, R T2 where T1/T2 are subterms of T; the guard condition should be satisfied as both are on strict subterms. In `R`, the call RV T is on the same T as the argument — same struct argument, not a subterm, but it's the same argument which is allowed in the same Fixpoint's recursion? For mutual fixpoints, the guard checker allows calls between the functions on the same (or smaller) arguments? The "struct" argument must decrease; calling RV T from R T is "the same" argument, which I believe is allowed for mutual fixpoints? Actually, in Coq, mutual fixpoints require each recursive call to have the recursive argument structurally smaller than the corresponding formal argument of the function being defined... For a call from R to RV, the struct argument of R is T and of RV is T — same term. I think Coq's guard checker rejects calls that are not on a strict subterm except in the same function (for the same function, it allows the same argument? no — a recursive call must be on a strict subterm, unless it's a "refolding" case). Hmm, actually Coq's guard condition: recursive calls must be on structurally smaller arguments, but for mutually recursive functions, the requirement is that the call decreases in some argument according to a global well-founded order; calling RV T from R T with the same T is generally accepted? Let me recall: In Coq, mutual fixpoints like

```
Fixpoint even (n : nat) {struct n} : bool :=
  match n with 0 => true | S k => odd k end
with odd (n : nat) {struct n} : bool :=
  match n with 0 => false | S k => even k end.
```
Here even (S k) calls odd k — strict subterm ✓. Calling `odd n` from `even n` (same n) would be rejected as not decreasing. So R calling RV T (same T) might be rejected.

To avoid this, define R and RV in a single function by a "fuel" parameter? Or define R via RV with a separate Fixpoint: R can be a Definition after RV if RV doesn't reference R. But RV's arrow case needs R on the codomain. We can inline the definition of R in RV's arrow case:

```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      match ir kappa with
      | High => True
      | Low => exists T1a b1 ka1 T1b b2 ka2, ... /\
            (forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T1 a1 a2 ->
               (forall u1 u2, evaluates (open b1 a1) u1 -> evaluates (open b2 a2) u2 -> RV T2 u1 u2))
      end
  end.

Definition R (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2.
```

The inline R T2 = forall u1 u2, evaluates → RV T2 u1 u2, with T2 a strict subterm of T ✓. Then R is a plain Definition. This works! Slightly verbose but fine. In proofs, we can fold/unfold.

Actually, we can define R after RV and use `R` in RV's arrow case? No, order matters; but we can define a notation or just inline. Alternatively, define RV with a parameter for R impossible... Let's inline and then prove `R` unfold lemma. In the arrow case, the inner forall-evaluates is exactly `R T2` unfolded. We can write the definition as the inlined form; then when reasoning, `unfold R` gives the same form, so they match (`change`/`fold`).

The function subst_all recursively maps fvar y ↦ env' y. Opening inserts fvar x (the *same atom* as the binder name). If body has free occurrences of fvar x, they are also mapped to env' x = a. RHS = open (subst_all env body) (env' x): the free occurrences of fvar x in body are mapped by env (not env') to env x, which may differ from a. So we need x ∉ fv body OR env x = a. Choosing x fresh from fv body is the standard cofinite approach ✓.

Now, there's another subtlety: the substitution lemma's RHS is `open (subst_all env body) (env' x)`. But env' x = a; and the LHS's subst_all env' applied to the *opened* fvar x gives a. ✓.

Hmm, and one more: subst_all env' (open body (fvar x)) — the subst_all traversal goes under binders; the opened position is at binder depth 0 of body (i.e., the outermost binder of body when body is opened). Since body may itself contain binders, the opened fvar x is not under any of them (unless body's bvar 0 occurs under an inner binder as bvar 1, handled by open_rec's k increment). After opening, fvar x may occur under binders inside body; subst_all replaces it with a, which is lc so no capture ✓.

Proving `subst_plug` by induction on C. C has embedded terms; the induction needs x ∉ fv of those embedded terms. Define `fv_context` accordingly:
```
Fixpoint fv_context C :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' k => fv_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t r => fv_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' r => fv t ++ fv_context C'
  ...
```
Then the proof: induction on C; the hole: subst x u (tm_fvar x) = u (if x=?x true); the other cases: simpl; the substitution distributes over the constructors; the binders (C_Abs, C_CaseLeft, C_CaseRight) involve `subst x u` under a binder — because plug puts the hole under the binder, and subst x u recurses under binders with `subst` (which doesn't shift since x is an fvar and u is lc) ✓. The induction works.

Also, we need `subst_all sigma (plug C (fvar x))` with sigma = fun y => if y =? x then t else fvar y. Next, the substitution and free variables.

Define subst_all:
```coq
Fixpoint subst_all (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_unit kappa => tm_unit kappa
  | tm_abs T body kappa => tm_abs T (subst_all sigma body) kappa
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_all sigma t1) (subst_all sigma t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 kappa => tm_pair (subst_all sigma t1) (subst_all sigma t2) kappa
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_all sigma t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_all sigma t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 kappa => tm_inl (subst_all sigma t1) kappa
  | tm_inr t1 kappa => tm_inr (subst_all sigma t1) kappa
  | tm_case t0 body1 body2 r => tm_case (subst_all sigma t0) (subst_all sigma body1) (subst_all sigma body2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_all sigma t1)
  | tm_if c yes no r => tm_if (subst_all sigma c) (subst_all sigma yes) (subst_all sigma no) r
  end.
```

Lemma subst_all_open_rec:
```coq
Lemma open_rec_no_bvar : forall t k u, lc_at 0 t -> open_rec k u t = t.
```
Hmm, more precisely: if t has no bvars at all (lc_at 0), then open_rec k u t = t for any k, u. Prove by induction on t; the bvar case: lc_at 0 (bvar i) means i < 0, impossible; destruct via lia. Need to handle the lc_at hypothesis which for compound terms is a conjunction or nested. Let me write:

```coq
Lemma open_rec_no_bvar : forall t k u, lc_at 0 t -> open_rec k u t = t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k u H; simpl in *; try reflexivity.
  - (* bvar *) lia.
  - (* abs *) simpl. f_equal. apply IHt. simpl in H. exact H.
  ...
```
Hmm, induction t with `simpl in *` may mangle. Let me write it carefully:
- tm_bvar i: lc_at 0 (bvar i) = (i < 0); lia.

Prove by induction on t with the depth generalized. This is the standard lemma. 

Actually careful: in `open_rec k u t`, under binders the depth increments, matching lc_at's increment ✓.

Then subst_all_open_rec:

Lemma subst_all_open_rec : forall t sigma k u,

So:
```coq
Lemma lc_at_le : forall t k k', k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
```
Prove by induction on t generally.

Lemma subst_all_agree : forall sigma tau t,

Since value is an inductive with premises that are values, `induction 1` gives IH for each premise of the constructor. For v_pair the IHs are exactly the statements for v1 and v2 (if we use `induction 1` with the goal `forall t, ~ (v --> t)` depending on v; the IH for the value premises ✓). Then inversion gives the step premise; eauto should apply the IH.

Careful: `inversion Hstep` where Hstep : tm_pair v1 v2 kappa --> t'. The ST_Pair1 constructor: t1 --> t1' with the conclusion tm_pair t1 t2 kappa --> tm_pair t1' t2 kappa. Unifying with tm_pair v1 v2 kappa gives t1 = v1, t2 = v2, t' = tm_pair t1' v2 kappa, and premise v1 --> t1'. Then apply IHvalue1 → contradiction ✓. Similarly inl/inr: ST_Inl gives v --> t' premise → IH ✓. And eauto with the IH should work.

Now the RV/R definitions and lemmas. Let me write them.

```coq
Definition sec_ir (T : ty) : label := (security_of T).(indirect_reader).

Fixpoint RV (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => (exists ka1, v1 = tm_unit ka1) /\ (exists ka2, v2 = tm_unit ka2)
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists u1 u2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inl u1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl u2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T1 u1 u2)
          \/
          (exists u1 u2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inr u1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr u2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T2 u1 u2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists u1 u2 ka1 ka2,
             v1 = tm_pair u1 u2 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair u1 u2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T1 u1 u2 /\ RV T2 u1 u2
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists T1a b1 ka1 T1b b2 ka2,
            v1 = tm_abs T1a b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T1b b2 ka2 /\
            ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
            (forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T1 a1 a2 ->
               forall u1 u2, evaluates (open b1 a1) u1 ->
                             evaluates (open b2 a2) u2 -> RV T2 u1 u2)
      end
  end.
```

Hmm, wait. For the product case, I wrote `v2 = tm_pair u1 u2 ka2` reusing u1 u2 — is that right? The pair components on the two sides are related at T1/T2 but need not be equal terms. Should be `v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\ RV T1 a1 a2 /\ RV T2 b1 b2`. Let me use distinct variables. Yes, of course.

Let me redo the product:
```
exists a1 b1 ka1 a2 b2 ka2,
  v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\
  ka1.ir = Low /\ ka2.ir = Low /\ RV T1 a1 a2 /\ RV T2 b1 b2
```

Similarly sums with distinct payloads ✓ as written.

Now, one question: for the strong arrow case, should the closure body relation quantify over `value a1` etc.? The a1,a2 in the closure relation are the runtime arguments; they're values ✓. Good.

```coq
Definition R (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2.
```

Now the subtype value lemma:
```coq
Lemma RV_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall v1 v2, RV T v1 v2 -> RV U v1 v2.
Proof.
  induction 1; simpl in *; intros v1 v2 H; try exact H; try (unfold RV...).
```
Careful: `induction 1` on subtype gives goals where simpl unfolds RV U... Let me write with explicit intros and case analysis.

For S_Unit: goal RV (Ty_Unit kappa2) v1 v2 given RV (Ty_Unit kappa1) and security_le kappa1 kappa2. Need to match on kappa2.ir: High → True; Low → shape. Given security_le: flows_to kappa1.ir kappa2.ir. If kappa2.ir = Low then flows_to kappa1.ir Low → kappa1.ir = Low (destruct kappa1.ir). So RV (Ty_Unit kappa1) = shape; and RV (Ty_Unit kappa2) = shape (same shape condition, no annotation requirement) ✓.

For S_Sum: given H : RV (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa1) v1 v2, need RV (Ty_Sum U1 U2 kappa2). Case kappa2.ir: High → True; Low → kappa1.ir Low (from security_le: flows_to kappa1.ir kappa2.ir) → unfold both; destruct H as [inl case | inr case]; construct corresponding with IHs on RV T1 U1 and RV T2 U2 (subderivations) ✓. But note the IHs from `induction 1` for S_Sum are the statements `forall v1 v2, RV T1 v1 v2 -> RV U1 v1 v2` ✓.

For S_Arrow: IHs: subtype U1 T1 (IH: RV U1 ⊆ RV T1) and subtype T2 U2 (IH: RV T2 ⊆ RV U2). Given H : RV (Arrow T1 T2 κ1). Need RV (Arrow U1 U2 κ2). If κ2.ir High → True. If Low: κ1.ir Low via security_le. Unfold H: closures + annotation lows + forall a1 a2, value -> value -> RV T1 a1 a2 -> R T2 (open b1 a1)(open b2 a2)... where R T2 unfolded. Need R U2: i.e., forall u1 u2, evaluates → RV U2. Given args related at U1: apply IH(U1,T1) to get RV T1; then H's forall gives the evaluates-to-RV T2; then IH(T2,U2) gives RV U2 ✓.

For S_Trans: IHs compose ✓.

Now, the term-level:
```coq
Lemma R_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall t1 t2, R T t1 t2 -> R U t1 t2.
Proof. intros T U Hsub; induction Hsub; intros t1 t2 H; ... 
```
Hmm, simpler: unfold R; intros; apply RV_subtype with T; auto. Actually:
```coq
Lemma R_subtype : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall t1 t2, R T t1 t2 -> R U t1 t2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hsub t1 t2 H v1 v2 E1 E2.
  apply (RV_subtype T U Hsub v1 v2).
  exact (H v1 v2 E1 E2).
Qed.
```

Now the evaluation inversion lemmas. Let me write them one by one in Coq.

Before that, we need `multi` congruence lemmas? Not really; the inversions need induction on multi. Let me write a general "decomposition" pattern.

Actually, a neat trick: prove a generic lemma that multi can be decomposed as a sequence of steps, and use it to invert. But induction on multi with remembering the term is the standard approach.

Let me write `ev_app`:

```coq
Lemma ev_app : forall t1 t2 r v,
  evaluates (tm_app t1 t2 r) v ->
  exists w1 a, evaluates t1 w1 /\ evaluates t2 a /\
    exists T1a body ka, w1 = tm_abs T1a body ka /\
      evaluates (tm_protect ka.(indirect_reader) (open body a)) v.
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r v [Hm Hval].
  remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as s eqn:E.
  revert t1 t2 r E.
  induction Hm; intros t1 t2 r E; subst.
  - (* multi_refl *) inversion Hval.
  - (* multi_step s u Hstep Hm *) inversion Hstep; subst; clear Hstep.
    + (* ST_AppAbs *) inversion E... 
```
Hmm, let me be careful: `induction Hm` where Hm : multi (tm_app t1 t2 r) v after remember. Actually `remember ... as s eqn:E` gives Hm : multi s v and E : s = tm_app t1 t2 r. Then `revert t1 t2 r E; induction Hm` — Hm's statement is multi s v with s fixed; the induction principle: for multi_refl s, goal forall t1 t2 r, s = tm_app t1 t2 r -> ...; for multi_step s u v Hstep Hm, IH about multi u v: `forall t1 t2 r, u = tm_app t1 t2 r -> ...`.

Case refl: E : s = tm_app t1 t2 r, and Hval : value v; but Hm : multi s v = multi (tm_app t1 t2 r) v with s = ...; wait, in the refl case v = s, so Hval : value s = value (tm_app t1 t2 r) — contradiction with pair? Need a lemma that tm_app is not a value. Inversion on Hval with Hm=E: after subst E, Hval : value (tm_app t1 t2 r); inversion Hval closes ✓ (value has no app constructor).

Case step: Hstep : step s u; E : s = tm_app t1 t2 r. subst s. Invert Hstep:
- ST_AppAbs: premise value (tm_abs T body kappa), value v0, flows_to..., and t1 = tm_abs T body kappa, t2 = v0, u = tm_protect kappa.ir (open body v0). Result: exists w1 a: w1 = tm_abs T body kappa, a = v0. evaluates t1 w1 ✓ (refl, value); evaluates t2 a ✓ (refl, value). Exists T, body, kappa ✓. And evaluates (tm_protect kappa.ir (open body v0)) v: from Hm : multi u v and Hval ✓.
- ST_App1: t1 --> t1', u = tm_app t1' t2 r. IH Hm applied to t1', t2, r with E = refl gives (w1, a, Ev1, Ev2, T1a, body, ka, Eq, Ev3). Then exists w1,a; evaluates t1 w1 via multi_step Hstep' Ev1 (where Hstep' : step t1 t1'); others ✓.
- ST_App2: value t1, t2 --> t2', u = tm_app t1 t2' r. IH gives w1, a, Ev1 : evaluates t1 w1, Ev2 : evaluates t2' a, ...; then evaluates t2 a via multi_step Hstep' Ev2 ✓.

Need the `inversion Hstep` to properly handle term equalities. Let me just write it and let Coq tell me.

Lemma ev_case : forall t b1 b2 r v,

Lemma ev_if : forall c y n r v,

Steps: ST_If (c --> c'), ST_IfTrue (c = tm_inl v0 k, value v0, flows; result tm_protect k.ir yes), ST_IfFalse.

Then:
```coq
Lemma protect_value_low : forall v, protect_value v Low = v.
Proof. destruct v; simpl; destruct s as [r i]; destruct r,i; reflexivity. Qed.
```
Careful: protect_value matches on 5 constructors with security args; the default case returns v. For tm_abs T body kappa: protect_value = tm_abs T body (protect_security kappa Low); need = tm_abs T body kappa; destruct kappa's fields. For pair: tm_pair v1 v2 (protect_security kappa Low) = same as long as the annotation reduces — but the pair's annotation is inside; destruct the security. ✓.

```coq
Lemma ev_protect_low_iff : forall t v, evaluates (tm_protect Low t) v <-> evaluates t v.
Proof.
  intros t v; split.
  - intros [Hm Hv]. destruct (ev_protect Low t v (conj Hm Hv)) as (u & Eu & Hv_eq).
    rewrite protect_value_low in Hv_eq. subst v. exact Eu.
  - intros Eu. destruct Eu as [Hm Hv]. split; [|exact Hv].
    assert (Hprot : multi (tm_protect Low t) (tm_protect Low v)).
    { clear Hm; induction Hm... } 
```
Need multi_protect:
```coq
Lemma multi_protect : forall l t u, multi t u -> multi (tm_protect l t) (tm_protect l u).
Proof.
  intros l t u Hm; induction Hm.
  - apply multi_refl.
  - eapply multi_step. apply ST_Protect; eauto. exact IHHm.
Qed.
```
Then tm_protect Low v --> protect_value v Low (ST_ProtectValue, value v) and protect_value v Low = v; so multi (tm_protect Low t) v via Hprot then multi_step with ST_ProtectValue then multi_refl ✓.

```coq
Fixpoint all_ir_low (T : ty) : Prop :=
  (security_of T).(indirect_reader) = Low /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ => all_ir_low T1 /\ all_ir_low T2
  end.
```
and prove transparent_at kappa T -> kappa.ir = Low -> all_ir_low T by induction on T ✓.

Then the erase lemma:
```coq
Lemma RV_erase : forall kappa T v1 v2,
  ground T -> transparent_at kappa T -> kappa.(indirect_reader) = Low ->
  RV T v1 v2 -> erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.
```
By induction on T; ground gives structure:
- Ty_Unit: all_ir_low gives T.ir Low → RV T = shapes (v1 = tm_unit ka1, v2 = tm_unit ka2) → erase both tm_unit public ✓.
- Ty_Sum T1 T2 k: all_ir_low gives k.ir Low → RV strong sum: same side (inl case): v1 = tm_inl u1 ka1, v2 = tm_inl u2 ka2, RV T1 u1 u2. Ground: ground T1 ✓. transparent_at kappa T gives transparent_at kappa T1 ✓. IH T1 gives erase u1 = erase u2. Then erase (tm_inl u1 ka1) = tm_inl (erase u1) public, similarly ✓. Need erase_security (tm_inl u ka) = tm_inl (erase_security u) public ✓ simpl.
- Ty_Prod similarly.
- Ty_Arrow: ground = False → contradiction.

Now the final target theorem's proof structure in detail:

```coq
Theorem noninterference : forall (C : program_context) (t1 t2 : tm) (T_secret T_result : ty),
  has_type empty t1 T_secret ->
  has_type empty t2 T_secret ->
  context_has_type C T_secret T_result ->
  ground T_result ->
  transparent T_result ->
  ~ flows_to (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)
      (security_of T_result).(indirect_reader) ->
  same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2).
Proof.
  intros C t1 t2 T_secret T_result Ht1 Ht2 Hctx Hground Htrans Hnflow.
  (* derive secret_ir = High *)
  assert (Hsec : (security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader) = High).
  { destruct ((security_of T_secret).(indirect_reader)) eqn:E; auto.
    exfalso; apply Hnflow. destruct ((security_of T_result).(indirect_reader)); simpl; auto. }
```
Hmm: if secret_ir = Low, then flows_to Low result_ir is True by definition. We want to show flows_to Low result_ir; destruct result_ir; simpl; auto. But careful: flows_to Low High = True ✓ (match Low, High => True). ✓.

flows_to High High = True ✓.

Fresh atom choice: need some x not in L. Since atoms are nat, define fresh. Actually we can simply use `S (fold_right max 0 L)`. Let me define:

```coq
Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right max 0 L).

Lemma in_le_fold_max : forall L a, In a L -> a <= fold_right max 0 L.
Proof.
  induction L as [|b L IH]; intros a Hin; simpl in *; [contradiction|].
  apply in_inv in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
  - apply Nat.le_max_l.
  - eapply le_trans; [apply IH; eassumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh in Hin. pose proof (in_le_fold_max L (S (fold_right max 0 L)) Hin). lia.
Qed.
```
Check `in_inv` from Lists.List: `in_inv : forall (A : Type) (x : A) (l : list A), In x l -> (exists l', l = x :: l') \/ (exists l', l = x :: l' ...)`. Hmm, in Coq's List, `in_inv` states: `In a l -> a = h \/ In a t` for l = h :: t. The lemma name might be `in_inv` with statement `forall (A : Type) (x : A) (l : list A), In x l -> (exists l', l = x :: l') \/ (exists l', l = x :: l' ...)`. Let me just use `destruct Hin as [-> | Hin]` after `simpl in Hin` since In a (b :: L) reduces to a = b \/ In a L ✓ definitionally. So:
```coq
Lemma in_le_fold_max : forall L a, In a L -> a <= fold_right max 0 L.
Proof.
  induction L as [|b L IH]; intros a Hin; simpl in *.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply le_trans; [apply IH; eassumption|apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.
```
Good.

Now, a concern about fold_right max 0 with ListNotations imported: `fold_right` is from List (stdlib). Since `From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List`, `fold_right` is available. Note in Rocq 9.1, List is in Stdlib.Lists.List. OK.

Chunk 2:

```coq
(* ---------- Substitution of all free variables ---------- *)

Fixpoint subst_all (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_unit kappa => tm_unit kappa
  | tm_abs T body kappa => tm_abs T (subst_all sigma body) kappa
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_all sigma t1) (subst_all sigma t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 kappa => tm_pair (subst_all sigma t1) (subst_all sigma t2) kappa
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_all sigma t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_all sigma t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 kappa => tm_inl (subst_all sigma t1) kappa
  | tm_inr t1 kappa => tm_inr (subst_all sigma t1) kappa
  | tm_case t0 body1 body2 r =>
      tm_case (subst_all sigma t0) (subst_all sigma body1) (subst_all sigma body2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_all sigma t1)
  | tm_if c yes no r =>
      tm_if (subst_all sigma c) (subst_all sigma yes) (subst_all sigma no) r
  end.

Lemma lc_at_mono : forall t k k', k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  induction t; intros k k' Hle H; simpl in *; auto; try lia.
  - apply IHt; [lia | exact H].
  ...
```

Let me carefully write lc_at_mono:
- tm_bvar i: H : i < k, goal i < k'; lia.
- tm_fvar, tm_unit: True.
- tm_abs T body kappa: goal lc_at (S k') body; IHt with (S k) (S k'): S k ≤ S k' from Hle; H : lc_at (S k) body ✓.
- tm_app t1 t2 r: goal conjunction; split; [apply IHt1 with k|apply IHt2 with k]; lia/assumption.
- tm_pair same.
- tm_fst, tm_snd, tm_inl, tm_inr, tm_protect: single subterm.
- tm_case t0 b1 b2 r: H : lc_at k t0 /\ lc_at (S k) b1 /\ lc_at (S k) b2; goal lc_at k' t0 /\ lc_at (S k') b1 /\ lc_at (S k') b2.
- tm_if c y n r: like app with three.

Hmm, `induction 1` — the value inductive. For v_abs, no IHs. ```coq
Lemma value_no_step : forall v, value v -> forall t, ~ (v --> t).

First, the RV/R definitions. Let me write them carefully.

```coq
(* ---------- The logical relation ---------- *)

Fixpoint RV (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low => (exists ka1, v1 = tm_unit ka1) /\ (exists ka2, v2 = tm_unit ka2)
      end
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          (exists a1 ka1 a2 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inl a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inl a2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T1 a1 a2)
          \/
          (exists a1 ka1 a2 ka2,
             v1 = tm_inr a1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_inr a2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T2 a1 a2)
      end
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists a1 b1 ka1 a2 b2 ka2,
             v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             RV T1 a1 a2 /\ RV T2 b1 b2
      end
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa =>
      match kappa.(indirect_reader) with
      | High => True
      | Low =>
          exists T1a b1 ka1 T1b b2 ka2,
             v1 = tm_abs T1a b1 ka1 /\ v2 = tm_abs T1b b2 ka2 /\
             ka1.(indirect_reader) = Low /\ ka2.(indirect_reader) = Low /\
             (forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T1 a1 a2 ->
                forall u1 u2, evaluates (open b1 a1) u1 ->
                              evaluates (open b2 a2) u2 -> RV T2 u1 u2)
      end
  end.
```

Also `evaluates` must be defined before RV ✓ (it is).

Actually, there might be a neater way to do the inversions: since `multi_step` chains steps, and the step constructors for a given term shape are limited, I can prove a general "decomposition" lemma by induction on multi with the initial term generalized. Let me write each.

ev_app:
```coq
Lemma ev_app : forall t1 t2 r v,
  evaluates (tm_app t1 t2 r) v ->
  exists w1 a, evaluates t1 w1 /\ evaluates t2 a /\
    exists T1a body ka, w1 = tm_abs T1a body ka /\
      evaluates (tm_protect ka.(indirect_reader) (open body a)) v.
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 r v [Hm Hv].
  remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as s eqn:E.
  revert t1 t2 r E.
  induction Hm; intros t1 t2 r E.
  - subst s. simpl in Hv. inversion Hv.
  - subst s. inversion H; subst; clear H.
    + (* ST_AppAbs *)
      exists (tm_abs T body kappa), v0.
      split; [apply evaluates_value; assumption|].
      split; [apply evaluates_value; assumption|].
      exists T, body, kappa. split; [reflexivity|].
      split; [exact Hm|exact Hv].
    + (* ST_App1 *)
      edestruct IHHm as (w1 & a & E1 & E2 & T1a & body & ka & Eq & E3);
        [reflexivity|].
      exists w1, a. split; [|split]; [|exact E2|exact E3|].
      * eapply multi_step; [eassumption | exact E1].
      * exists T1a, body, ka. split; [exact Eq | exact E3].
    + (* ST_App2 *)
      edestruct IHHm as (w1 & a & E1 & E2 & T1a & body & ka & Eq & E3);
        [reflexivity|].
      exists w1, a. split; [|split]; [exact E1| |].
      * eapply multi_step; [eassumption | exact E2].
      * exists T1a, body, ka. split; [exact Eq | exact E3].
Qed.
```
Hmm, the induction's `Hm` in the step case: after `induction Hm`, the step case has `H : step s u` and `Hm : multi u v` (tail), and the IH is about multi u v with the revert. Let me carefully structure: `remember (tm_app t1 t2 r) as s eqn:E. revert t1 t2 r E. induction Hm; intros t1 t2 r E.` In the step case, Hm : multi s v where s is the remembered term; the step hypothesis is `H : step s u`? The names depend. Then `subst s` substitutes s = tm_app t1 t2 r into H and the goal; then `inversion H`.

But wait: after `revert t1 t2 r E; induction Hm`, in the step case the IH is `forall t1 t2 r, u = tm_app t1 t2 r -> exists ...` — actually the IH is over the tail `multi u v` with the equation for u. Hmm, the revert included E which mentions s; the induction hypothesis is generated for the tail with the same motive, i.e., `IHHm : forall t1 t2 r, u = tm_app t1 t2 r -> exists...`? Let me trust and compile.

Actually, there's a subtlety: `remember X as s eqn:E` where X = tm_app t1 t2 r; then Hm : multi s v. `revert t1 t2 r E` — E : s = tm_app t1 t2 r. The motive of induction is fun s => forall t1 t2 r, s = tm_app t1 t2 r -> P s v (where P includes the existential). Actually Hm's type is multi s v; revert t1 t2 r E generalizes the goal to `forall t1 t2 r, s = tm_app t1 t2 r -> ...`, with s still in the context? Hmm, reverting E (which contains s) will also revert s? E : s = tm_app t1 t2 r depends on s. Reverting E after t1,t2,r... ```coq
Lemma R_protect_low : forall T t1 t2, R T t1 t2 -> R T (tm_protect Low t1) (tm_protect Low t2).

  intros T t1 t2 H v1 v2 E1 E2.

  - apply ev_protect_low_iff. exact E2.

  - apply ev_protect_low_iff. exact E2.
Qed.

Excellent! All the evaluation decomposition lemmas compile. Let me verify the file still compiles fully (it does, since the target theorem is still Admitted). Let me now tackle the fundamental theorem.

Fundamental theorem statement:

```coq
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma1 sigma2 : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma1 y)) /\
  (forall y, locally_closed (sigma2 y)) /\
  (forall x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> R T (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x)).

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2,
    env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
    R T (subst_all sigma1 t) (subst_all sigma2 t).
```

Proof by induction on the typing derivation. This is the biggest chunk. Let me think through each case in Coq detail.

I'll write `intros Gamma t T H. induction H as [...]` — has_type has 13 constructors; I need the as-pattern. Let me determine the constructor argument lists:

1. T_Var : forall Gamma x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> wf_ty T -> has_type Gamma (tm_fvar x) T.
   Args: Gamma x T Hlook Hwf. No recursive. Pattern: `Gamma x T Hlook Hwf`.
2. T_Unit : forall Gamma kappa, wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_unit kappa) (Ty_Unit kappa).
   Pattern: `Gamma kappa Hwf`.
3. T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 body T2 kappa, wf_ty T1 -> wf_security kappa -> (forall x, ~In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 body kappa) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa).
   Pattern: `L Gamma T1 body T2 kappa HwfT Hwfk Hprem IHprem`.
4. T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r, has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) -> has_type Gamma t2 T1 -> flows_to kappa.reader r -> has_type Gamma (tm_app t1 t2 r) (ty_protect kappa.ir T2).
   Pattern: `Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hflow`.
5. T_Pair : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa, has_type Gamma t1 T1 -> has_type Gamma t2 T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_pair t1 t2 kappa) (Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa).
   Pattern: `Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hwfk`.
6. T_Fst : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r, has_type Gamma t (Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa) -> flows_to kappa.reader r -> has_type Gamma (tm_fst t r) (ty_protect kappa.ir T1).
   Pattern: `Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow`.
7. T_Snd : same with T2: `Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow`.
8. T_Inl : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa, has_type Gamma t T1 -> wf_ty T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_inl t kappa) (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa).
   Pattern: `Gamma t T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 HwfT2 Hwfk`.
9. T_Inr : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa, wf_ty T1 -> has_type Gamma t T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_inr t kappa) (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa).
   Pattern: `Gamma t T1 T2 kappa HwfT1 H2 IH2 Hwfk`.
10. T_Case : forall (L : list atom) Gamma t body1 body2 T1 T2 T kappa r, has_type Gamma t (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) -> flows_to kappa.reader r -> (forall x, ~In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body1 (tm_fvar x)) T) -> (forall x, ~In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T2) (open body2 (tm_fvar x)) T) -> has_type Gamma (tm_case t body1 body2 r) (ty_protect kappa.ir T).
    Pattern: `L Gamma t body1 body2 T1 T2 T0 kappa r Ht IHt Hflow Hp1 IHp1 Hp2 IHp2`.
11. T_Protect : forall Gamma t T l, has_type Gamma t T -> has_type Gamma (tm_protect l t) (ty_protect l T).
    Pattern: `Gamma t T0 l H1 IH1`.
12. T_Sub : forall Gamma t T U, has_type Gamma t T -> subtype T U -> has_type Gamma t U.
    Pattern: `Gamma t T0 U H1 IH1 Hsub`.
13. T_If : forall Gamma c yes no k T r, has_type Gamma c (Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k) -> has_type Gamma yes T -> has_type Gamma no T -> flows_to k.reader r -> has_type Gamma (tm_if c yes no r) (ty_protect k.ir T).
    Pattern: `Gamma c yes no k T0 r Hc IHc Hy IHy Hn IHn Hflow`.

Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else sigma y.

Lemma extend_eq : forall sigma x u, extend sigma x u x = u.
Lemma extend_neq : forall sigma x u y, y <> x -> extend sigma x u y = sigma y.
Lemma extend_lc : forall sigma x u, (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) -> locally_closed u -> forall y, locally_closed (extend sigma x u y).
Lemma extend_agree : forall sigma x u t, ~ In x (fv t) -> subst_all (extend sigma x u) t = subst_all sigma t.
```
extend_agree follows from subst_all_agree: for y ∈ fv t, y ≠ x (since x ∉ fv t), so extend sigma x u y = sigma y ✓.

Let me write the helper lemmas first:

```coq
Lemma lookup_context_update_eq : forall Gamma x T,
  lookup_context x (update Gamma x T) = Some T.
Lemma lookup_context_update_neq : forall Gamma x y T,
  y <> x -> lookup_context y (update Gamma x T) = lookup_context y Gamma.
```

```coq
Definition extend (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else sigma y.

Lemma extend_eq : forall sigma x u, extend sigma x u x = u.
Proof. intros; unfold extend; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma extend_neq : forall sigma x u y, y <> x -> extend sigma x u y = sigma y.
Proof. intros; unfold extend; destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction | reflexivity]. Qed.

Lemma extend_lc : forall sigma x u, (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) -> locally_closed u -> forall y, locally_closed (extend sigma x u y).
Proof.
  intros sigma x u Hsig Hu y. unfold extend.
  destruct (Nat.eqb y x); [exact Hu | apply Hsig].
Qed.

Lemma extend_agree : forall sigma x u t, ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst_all (extend sigma x u) t = subst_all sigma t.
Proof.
  intros. apply subst_all_agree. intros y Hy.
  symmetry. apply extend_neq. intro E; subst y; contradiction.
Qed.
```
Careful: subst_all_agree requires forall y, In y (fv t) -> sigma1 y = sigma2 y; with sigma1 = extend sigma x u, sigma2 = sigma; need extend ... y = sigma y; from ~In x (fv t) and Hy, y ≠ x ✓.

Also a lemma for the extended environment relation:
```coq
Lemma env_rel_extend : forall Gamma sigma1 sigma2 x T a1 a2,
  env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
  value a1 -> value a2 -> RV T a1 a2 ->
  env_rel (update Gamma x T) (extend sigma1 x a1) (extend sigma2 x a2).
Proof.
  intros Gamma sigma1 sigma2 x T a1 a2 [Hlc1 [Hlc2 Hrel]] Hv1 Hv2 HRV.
  split; [|split].
  - apply extend_lc; [exact Hlc1 | apply value_lc; exact Hv1].
  - apply extend_lc; [exact Hlc2 | apply value_lc; exact Hv2].
  - intros y U Hy. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst y.
      assert (U = T) by (rewrite lookup_context_update_eq in Hy; injection Hy; auto).
      subst U. rewrite extend_eq, extend_eq.
      apply R_of_RV; assumption.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E.
      rewrite lookup_context_update_neq in Hy by exact E.
      rewrite extend_neq by exact E. rewrite extend_neq by exact E.
      apply Hrel. exact Hy.
Qed.
```
Wait, in the y=x case, Hy : lookup_context x (update Gamma x T) = Some U; rewrite lookup_context_update_eq gives Some T = Some U → U = T ✓ via injection.

R_of_RV:
```coq
Lemma R_of_RV : forall T v1 v2, value v1 -> value v2 -> RV T v1 v2 -> R T v1 v2.
Proof.
  intros T v1 v2 Hv1 Hv2 HRV w1 w2 E1 E2.
  destruct E1 as [Hm1 _]. destruct E2 as [Hm2 _].
  assert (w1 = v1) by (apply multi_value_eq with (t := v1); assumption).
  assert (w2 = v2) by (apply multi_value_eq with (t := v2); assumption).
  subst w1; subst w2. exact HRV.
Qed.
```

Also, we might need the converse: values are lc to construct R... fine.

Lemma abs_no_step : forall T body kappa t, ~ (tm_abs T body kappa --> t).
Proof. intros; inversion 1. Qed.
Lemma unit_no_step : forall kappa t, ~ (tm_unit kappa --> t).
Proof. intros; inversion 1. Qed.
Lemma multi_abs_eq : forall T body kappa v, multi (tm_abs T body kappa) v -> v = tm_abs T body kappa.

Let me write the theorem:

```coq
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma1 sigma2, env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
  R T (subst_all sigma1 t) (subst_all sigma2 t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [ Gamma x T0 Hlook Hwf
                  | Gamma kappa Hwf
                  | L Gamma T1 body T2 kappa HwfT Hwfk Hprem IHprem
                  | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hflow
                  | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hwfk
                  | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow
                  | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow
                  | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 HwfT2 Hwfk
                  | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa HwfT1 H2 IH2 Hwfk
                  | L Gamma t body1 body2 T1 T2 T0 kappa r Ht IHt Hflow Hp1 IHp1 Hp2 IHp2
                  | Gamma t T0 l H1 IH1
                  | Gamma t T0 U H1 IH1 Hsub
                  | Gamma c yes no k T0 r Hc IHc Hy IHy Hn IHn Hflow ];
  intros sigma1 sigma2 Henv.
  ...
```

I'll write it case by case, with the as-pattern. Let me draft the whole proof carefully.

```coq
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
    has_type Gamma t T ->
    forall sigma1 sigma2, env_rel Gamma sigma1 sigma2 ->
    R T (subst_all sigma1 t) (subst_all sigma2 t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [ Gamma x T0 Hlook Hwf
                 | Gamma kappa Hwf
                 | L Gamma T1 body T2 kappa HwfT Hwfk Hprem IHprem
                 | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hflow
                 | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 H2 IH2 Hwfk
                 | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow
                 | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r H1 IH1 Hflow
                 | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa H1 IH1 HwfT2 Hwfk
                 | Gamma t T1 T2 kappa HwfT1 H2 IH2 Hwfk
                 | L Gamma t body1 body2 T1 T2 T0 kappa r Ht IHt Hflow Hp1 IHp1 Hp2 IHp2
                 | Gamma t T0 l H1 IH1
                 | Gamma t T0 U H1 IH1 Hsub
                 | Gamma c yes no k T0 r Hc IHc Hy IHy Hn IHn Hflow ];
  intros sigma1 sigma2 Henv.
```
Hmm, the as-pattern is long. Potential mismatch issues. Let me instead use `induction H` without as-pattern and inspect: no, too many names.

I'll go with the as-pattern and fix errors.

Wait, I need to double check the has_type constructor argument lists from the file (lines 293-338):

```
| T_Var : forall Gamma x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> wf_ty T -> has_type Gamma (tm_fvar x) T
| T_Unit : forall Gamma kappa, wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_unit kappa) (Ty_Unit kappa)
| T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 body T2 kappa, wf_ty T1 -> wf_security kappa -> (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 body kappa) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa)
| T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r, has_type Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 kappa) -> has_type Gamma t2 T1 -> flows_to kappa.(reader) r -> has_type Gamma (tm_app t1 t2 r) (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T2)
| T_Pair : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa, has_type Gamma t1 T1 -> has_type Gamma t2 T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_pair t1 t2 kappa) (Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa)
| T_Fst : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa r, has_type Gamma t (Ty_Prod T1 T2 kappa) -> flows_to kappa.(reader) r -> has_type Gamma (tm_fst t r) (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T1)
| T_Snd : ... (ty_protect kappa.ir T2)
| T_Inl : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa, has_type Gamma t T1 -> wf_ty T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_inl t kappa) (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa)
| T_Inr : forall Gamma t T1 T2 kappa, wf_ty T1 -> has_type Gamma t T2 -> wf_security kappa -> has_type Gamma (tm_inr t kappa) (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa)
| T_Case : forall (L : list atom) Gamma t body1 body2 T1 T2 T kappa r, has_type Gamma t (Ty_Sum T1 T2 kappa) -> flows_to kappa.(reader) r -> (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open body1 (tm_fvar x)) T) -> (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T2) (open body2 (tm_fvar x)) T) -> has_type Gamma (tm_case t body1 body2 r) (ty_protect kappa.(indirect_reader) T)
| T_Protect : forall Gamma t T l, has_type Gamma t T -> has_type Gamma (tm_protect l t) (ty_protect l T)
| T_Sub : forall Gamma t T U, has_type Gamma t T -> subtype T U -> has_type Gamma t U
| T_If : forall Gamma c yes no k T r, has_type Gamma c (Ty_Sum (Ty_Unit public) (Ty_Unit public) k) -> has_type Gamma yes T -> has_type Gamma no T -> flows_to k.(reader) r -> has_type Gamma (tm_if c yes no r) (ty_protect k.(indirect_reader) T)
```

So 13 constructors ✓, my pattern list matches with T0 for the T vars to avoid clashing with the outer T.

Behavior proof:
```

First, fv_context and plug substitution:
```coq
Fixpoint fv_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs T C' kappa => fv_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t2 r => fv_context C' ++ fv t2
  | C_AppRight t1 C' r => fv t1 ++ fv_context C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t2 kappa => fv_context C' ++ fv t2
  | C_PairRight t1 C' kappa => fv t1 ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Fst C' r => fv_context C'
  | C_Snd C' r => fv_context C'
  | C_Inl C' kappa => fv_context C'
  | C_Inr C' kappa => fv_context C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' body1 body2 r => fv_context C' ++ fv body1 ++ fv body2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' body2 r => fv t0 ++ fv_context C' ++ fv body2
  | C_CaseRight t0 body1 C' r => fv t0 ++ fv body1 ++ fv_context C'
  | C_Protect l C' => fv_context C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n r => fv_context C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n r => fv c ++ fv_context C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' r => fv c ++ fv y ++ fv_context C'
  end.
```

Then the lemma:
```coq
Lemma subst_all_plug : forall C x u,
  ~ In x (fv_context C) ->
  locally_closed u ->
  subst_all (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else tm_fvar y) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C u.
```
Proof by induction on C. For each case, `simpl`. The hole: subst_all sigma (tm_fvar x) = sigma x = u (eqb_refl) and plug C_Hole u = u ✓.
Other cases: plug C' t produces the constructor with plug C' (fvar x) inside and embedded terms. subst_all sigma of the whole = constructor (subst_all sigma (plug C' (fvar x))) (subst_all sigma embedded)... 
For embedded terms t2 with x ∉ fv t2, subst_all sigma t2 = t2 via subst_all_fvar_self ✓.
The binders (C_Abs, C_CaseLeft, C_CaseRight): plug puts tm_abs T (plug C' (fvar x)) kappa; subst_all sigma recurses under the binder ✓ no shift needed since fvar substitution; and the IH gives subst_all sigma (plug C' (fvar x)) = plug C' u; so we get tm_abs T (plug C' u) kappa = plug (C_Abs T C' kappa) u ✓.
For C_CaseLeft t0 C' body2 r: plug = tm_case t0 (plug C' (fvar x)) body2 r; subst_all = tm_case (subst_all sigma t0) (subst_all sigma (plug C' x)) (subst_all sigma body2) r; we need = tm_case t0 (plug C' u) body2 r; use subst_all_fvar_self for t0 and body2 with their freshness from fv_context (C_CaseLeft ...) = fv t0 ++ fv_context C' ++ fv body2 ✓.

Hmm, wait: in this lemma, the substitution is `subst_all sigma (plug C (fvar x))` where plug C (fvar x) has the hole under binders of C. If u is not lc (contains bvars), plug C u places u under those binders — same syntactic placement as subst_all puts it (subst_all replaces the fvar x under the binders with u verbatim). Are they equal? subst_all replaces fvar x with u wherever it occurs, including under binders — without shifting u. plug C u also places u under binders without shifting. Let me write:
```coq
Lemma subst_all_plug : forall C x u,
    ~ In x (fv_context C) ->
    subst_all (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else tm_fvar y) (plug C (tm_fvar x)) =
    plug C u.
Proof.

Let me give a concise final summary of what was done and the key design decisions:

1. Auxiliary lemmas about label flow order, security protection, substitution/open commutation, local closedness, value inversion, and evaluation decomposition.

2. The logical relation: `RV T v1 v2` on values, indexed by type, "strong" (structural) when the type's indirect_reader is Low, trivial when High, and `R T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, evaluates t1 v1 -> evaluates t2 v2 -> RV T v1 v2`.

3. Subtyping is antitone w.r.t. observability: subtype T U -> RV T ⊆ RV U (so R T ⊆ R U).

4. Fundamental theorem: if Γ ⊢ t : T and environments σ1, σ2 are related at Γ (pointwise R at each variable, plus locally-closed values), then R T (t[σ1]) (t[σ2]).

5. Final: use context_has_type with a fresh variable for the hole, substitute t1/t2, and apply the ground+transparent erasure lemma to conclude same_result.

Let me present this succinctly.
